### What I expected to happen

I expected moto to match null values with `{"exists": true}` as this is the way AWS behaves.

### What actually happens

Moto doesn't seem to match null values with `{"exists": true}`.

### How to reproduce the issue

Using `python 3.9.7`, run the following tests using `boto3==1.22.4`, `botocore==1.25.4` and `moto==3.1.8`. The first test passes but the second one throws 
```
AssertionError: Lists differ: [{'foo': '123', 'bar': '123'}] != [{'foo': '123', 'bar': '123'}, {'foo': None, 'bar': '123'}]

Second list contains 1 additional elements.
First extra element 1:
{'foo': None, 'bar': '123'}

- [{'bar': '123', 'foo': '123'}]
+ [{'bar': '123', 'foo': '123'}, {'bar': '123', 'foo': None}]
```

```python
import json
import unittest

import boto3
from moto import mock_events, mock_logs
from moto.core import ACCOUNT_ID


class TestPattern(unittest.TestCase):
    def setUp(self):
        self.pattern = {
            "source": ["test-source"],
            "detail-type": ["test-detail-type"],
            "detail": {
                "foo": [{"exists": True}],
                "bar": [{"exists": True}],
            },
        }

    def test_aws_matches_none_with_exists_filter(self):
        events_client = boto3.client("events")
        response = events_client.test_event_pattern(
            EventPattern=json.dumps(self.pattern),
            Event=json.dumps(
                {
                    "id": "123",
                    "account": ACCOUNT_ID,
                    "time": "2022-05-12T11:40:30.872473Z",
                    "region": "eu-west-1",
                    "resources": [],
                    "source": "test-source",
                    "detail-type": "test-detail-type",
                    "detail": {"foo": None, "bar": "123"},
                }
            ),
        )
        self.assertTrue(response["Result"])

    @mock_events
    @mock_logs
    def test_moto_matches_none_with_exists_filter(self):
        logs_client = boto3.client("logs")
        log_group_name = "test-log-group"
        logs_client.create_log_group(logGroupName=log_group_name)

        events_client = boto3.client("events")
        event_bus_name = "test-event-bus"
        events_client.create_event_bus(Name=event_bus_name)

        rule_name = "test-event-rule"
        events_client.put_rule(
            Name=rule_name,
            State="ENABLED",
            EventPattern=json.dumps(self.pattern),
            EventBusName=event_bus_name,
        )

        events_client.put_targets(
            Rule=rule_name,
            EventBusName=event_bus_name,
            Targets=[
                {
                    "Id": "123",
                    "Arn": f"arn:aws:logs:eu-west-1:{ACCOUNT_ID}:log-group:{log_group_name}",
                }
            ],
        )

        events_client.put_events(
            Entries=[
                {
                    "EventBusName": event_bus_name,
                    "Source": "test-source",
                    "DetailType": "test-detail-type",
                    "Detail": json.dumps({"foo": "123", "bar": "123"}),
                },
                {
                    "EventBusName": event_bus_name,
                    "Source": "test-source",
                    "DetailType": "test-detail-type",
                    "Detail": json.dumps({"foo": None, "bar": "123"}),

            logs_client.filter_log_events(logGroupName=log_group_name)["events"],

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple shopping cart system. Your program should define a class named `ShoppingCart` with the following methods:

- `add_item(item_name, price, quantity)`: Adds an item to the cart with the specified name, price, and quantity. If the item already exists in the cart, update its quantity.
- `remove_item(item_name)`: Removes the specified item from the cart if it exists.
- `total_cost()`: Returns the total cost of all items in the cart.
- `items_in_cart()`: Returns a list of tuples, each containing the name, price, and quantity of an item in the cart.

**Input:**
- The input will be a series of commands to manipulate the shopping cart. Each command will be on a new line.
- Commands can be one of the following:
  - `add <item_name> <price> <quantity>`: Adds an item to the cart.
  - `remove <item_name>`: Removes an item from the cart.
  - `total`: Prints the total cost of the cart.
  - `list`: Prints the list of items in the cart.

**Output:**
- For each `total` command, print the total cost of the cart.
- For each `list` command, print the list of items in the cart in the format: `[(item_name, price, quantity), ...]`.

**Sample Input:**
```
add apple 1.5 10
add banana 0.5 5
list
total
remove apple
list
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[('apple', 1.5, 10), ('banana', 0.5, 5)]
17.5
[('banana', 0.5, 5)]
```

```python
class ShoppingCart:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty dictionary to store items."""
        self.items = {}

    def add_item(self, item_name, price, quantity):
        """Add or update the item in the cart."""
        if item_name in self.items:
            self.items[item_name]['quantity'] += quantity
        else:
            self.items[item_name] = {'price': price, 'quantity': quantity}

    def remove_item(self, item_name):
        """Remove the item from the cart if it exists."""
        if item_name in self.items:
            del self.items[item_name]

    def total_cost(self):
        """Calculate the total cost of all items in the cart."""
        total = 0
        for item in self.items.values():
            total += item['price'] * item['quantity']
        return total

    def items_in_cart(self):
        """Return a list of items in the cart with their details."""
        return [(item_name, item['price'], item['quantity']) for item_name, item in self.items.items()]

# Example usage:
# cart = ShoppingCart()
# cart.add_item("apple", 1.5, 10)
# cart.add_item("banana", 0.5, 5)
# print(cart.items_in_cart())  # Output: [('apple', 1.5, 10), ('banana', 0.5, 5)]
# print(cart.total_cost())	# Output: 17.5
# cart.remove_item("apple")
# print(cart.items_in_cart())  # Output: [('banana', 0.5, 5)]

<think>
Okay, so I need to evaluate the integral from 0 to π/2 of {tan x}/tan x dx, where {u} is the fractional part of u, which is u minus the floor of u. Hmm. Let me start by recalling what the fractional part function does. For any real number u, {u} = u - floor(u). So, for example, if u is between 0 and 1, the fractional part is just u itself because floor(u) would be 0. But once u is 1 or larger, the fractional part is u minus the integer part. So, in the case of tan x, I need to figure out when tan x is between 0 and 1, and when it's 1 or more, because that would affect the fractional part.

First, let's consider the behavior of tan x between 0 and π/2. At x = 0, tan x is 0. As x approaches π/2, tan x goes to infinity. So, tan x increases from 0 to infinity in the interval [0, π/2). Now, when is tan x equal to 1? That happens at x = π/4, since tan(π/4) = 1. So, from x = 0 to x = π/4, tan x is between 0 and 1. Therefore, in [0, π/4), {tan x} = tan x because it's already less than 1. Then, from π/4 to π/2, tan x is greater than or equal to 1, so {tan x} = tan x - floor(tan x). But floor(tan x) in this interval would be the integer part. Wait, but tan x is increasing from 1 to infinity here. So, in the interval [π/4, π/2), tan x starts at 1 and goes to infinity. Therefore, for each integer n ≥ 1, tan x will cross n at some point. So, the fractional part {tan x} will be tan x - n when tan x is in [n, n+1). 

Therefore, the integral can be split into two parts: from 0 to π/4, where {tan x}/tan x is just (tan x)/tan x = 1, so the integrand is 1. Then, from π/4 to π/2, the integrand becomes {tan x}/tan x = (tan x - floor(tan x))/tan x = 1 - floor(tan x)/tan x. So, the integral becomes the integral from 0 to π/4 of 1 dx plus the integral from π/4 to π/2 of [1 - floor(tan x)/tan x] dx.

But wait, maybe it's easier to split the integral into an infinite number of intervals where tan x is in [n, n+1), but since tan x is increasing, each interval in x corresponds to an interval in n. So, perhaps we can express the integral from π/4 to π/2 as a sum over n from 1 to infinity of the integral from x_n to x_{n+1} of [1 - n/tan x] dx, where x_n is the x such that tan x_n = n. Hmm, but integrating from π/4 to π/2, but tan x goes from 1 to infinity. So, for each integer n ≥ 1, x_n = arctan(n). Wait, but arctan(1) is π/4, arctan(2), arctan(3), etc., up to arctan(n) approaching π/2 as n approaches infinity. Therefore, the integral from π/4 to π/2 can be written as the sum from n=1 to infinity of the integral from arctan(n) to arctan(n+1) of [1 - n/tan x] dx. 

So, the entire integral would be the integral from 0 to π/4 of 1 dx plus the sum from n=1 to infinity of the integral from arctan(n) to arctan(n+1) of [1 - n/tan x] dx. Let's compute these one by one.

First part: integral from 0 to π/4 of 1 dx is simply π/4 - 0 = π/4.

Second part: the sum from n=1 to infinity of [ integral from arctan(n) to arctan(n+1) of (1 - n/tan x) dx ].

Let's compute the integral inside the sum for a general n. So, integral from arctan(n) to arctan(n+1) of [1 - n/tan x] dx. Let's split this into two integrals:

Integral of 1 dx from arctan(n) to arctan(n+1) is just the length of the interval, which is arctan(n+1) - arctan(n).

Then, the second term is -n times the integral from arctan(n) to arctan(n+1) of (1/tan x) dx. Now, 1/tan x is cot x. The integral of cot x dx is ln |sin x| + C. Therefore, the integral of cot x from a to b is ln(sin b) - ln(sin a).

Therefore, putting it all together, the integral for each n is [arctan(n+1) - arctan(n)] - n [ln(sin(arctan(n+1))) - ln(sin(arctan(n)))].

Hmm, now we need to simplify ln(sin(arctan(n))) and similar terms.

First, let's compute sin(arctan(n)). Let’s recall that if θ = arctan(n), then tan θ = n. So, we can imagine a right triangle where the opposite side is n and the adjacent side is 1, so the hypotenuse is sqrt(n^2 + 1). Therefore, sin θ = opposite/hypotenuse = n / sqrt(n^2 + 1). Similarly, sin(arctan(n)) = n / sqrt(n^2 + 1). Wait, actually, no. Let's verify.

If θ = arctan(n), then tan θ = n = opposite / adjacent. So, let me denote the opposite side as n and the adjacent as 1. Then hypotenuse is sqrt(n^2 + 1). Then, sin θ = opposite / hypotenuse = n / sqrt(n^2 + 1). Therefore, sin(arctan(n)) = n / sqrt(n^2 + 1). Similarly, sin(arctan(n+1)) = (n+1) / sqrt((n+1)^2 + 1) = (n+1)/sqrt(n^2 + 2n + 2).

Therefore, ln(sin(arctan(n+1))) - ln(sin(arctan(n))) = ln[(n+1)/sqrt((n+1)^2 + 1)] - ln[n/sqrt(n^2 + 1)].

Simplifying each term:

ln[(n+1)/sqrt((n+1)^2 + 1)] = ln(n+1) - (1/2)ln((n+1)^2 + 1)

Similarly, ln[n/sqrt(n^2 + 1)] = ln n - (1/2)ln(n^2 + 1)

- **Initialization**: Create a bank account with an account number, account holder's name, and an initial balance.
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The deposit amount must be positive.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance. The withdrawal amount must be positive and should not exceed the current balance.
- **Get Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.
- **String Representation**: Provide a string representation of the account details in the format: "Account: [account_number], Holder: [account_holder_name], Balance: [balance]".

**Constraints:**
- The account number is a string.
- The account holder's name is a string.
- The balance is a float.
- Deposit and withdrawal amounts are floats.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
account = BankAccount("123456789", "John Doe", 1000.0)
print(account)  # Output: Account: 123456789, Holder: John Doe, Balance: 1000.0
account.deposit(500.0)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 1500.0
account.withdraw(200.0)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 1300.0
account.withdraw(1500.0)  # Raises ValueError: Insufficient funds
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_number, account_holder_name, balance):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account.

        :param account_number: A string representing the account number.
        :param account_holder_name: A string representing the account holder's name.
        :param balance: A float representing the initial balance.
        """
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.account_holder_name = account_holder_name
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.

        :param amount: A float representing the amount to deposit.
        :raises ValueError: If the deposit amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.

        :param amount: A float representing the amount to withdraw.
        :raises ValueError: If the withdrawal amount is not positive or exceeds the current balance.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
            else:
                raise ValueError("Insufficient funds")
        else:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive")

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Get the current balance of the account.

        :return: A float representing the current balance.
        """
        return self.balance

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Provide a string representation of the account details.

        :return: A string in the format "Account: [account_number], Holder: [account_holder_name], Balance: [balance]".
        """
        return f"Account: {self.account_number}, Holder: {self.account_holder_name}, Balance: {self.balance}"
```<#sep#> + 1)))]

So, the entire second part of the integral is the sum from n=1 to infinity of this expression.

This seems quite complicated. Maybe there's a way to telescope the sum. Let's see.

First, let's separate the sum into two parts:

Sum [arctan(n+1) - arctan(n)] from n=1 to infinity.

This is a telescoping series. Let's compute it:

Sum from n=1 to N of [arctan(n+1) - arctan(n)] = arctan(N+1) - arctan(1). As N approaches infinity, arctan(N+1) approaches π/2, so the sum from n=1 to infinity is π/2 - arctan(1) = π/2 - π/4 = π/4.

Then, the second part of the sum is - sum from n=1 to infinity of n [ln((n+1)/n) - (1/2) ln(((n+1)^2 + 1)/(n^2 + 1)))].

So, the entire integral is π/4 (from the first part) plus π/4 (from the first sum) minus the sum from n=1 to infinity of [n ln((n+1)/n) - (n/2) ln(((n+1)^2 + 1)/(n^2 + 1)))].

Therefore, total integral is π/4 + π/4 - [sum terms] = π/2 - [sum terms].

So, the integral is π/2 minus the sum from n=1 to infinity of [n ln((n+1)/n) - (n/2) ln(((n+1)^2 + 1)/(n^2 + 1)))].

Now, we need to evaluate that sum. Let's denote S = sum_{n=1}^∞ [n ln((n+1)/n) - (n/2) ln(((n+1)^2 + 1)/(n^2 + 1)))].

Let me write each term as:

Term(n) = n ln((n+1)/n) - (n/2) ln[((n+1)^2 + 1)/(n^2 + 1)]

We can separate this into two sums:

S = sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln((n+1)/n) - (1/2) sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln[((n+1)^2 + 1)/(n^2 + 1)]

Let me tackle each sum separately.

S1 = sum_{n=1}^∞ n [ln(n+1) - ln(n)] = sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln(n+1) - n ln(n)

-1 ln(1) + (1 ln2 - 2 ln2) + (2 ln3 - 3 ln3) + (3 ln4 - 4 ln4) + ... )

The first term is n=1: 1(ln2 - ln1) = ln2 - 0 = ln2

n=4: 4(ln5 - ln4) = 4 ln5 - 4 ln4

The formula for the sum of a geometric series is S = a*(r^(n) -1)/(r-1), where a is the first term, r the ratio, and n the number of terms. <#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple stock trading simulator in Python. Your simulator should support the following operations:

1. **Buy**: Add a specified quantity of a stock to the portfolio at a given price.
2. **Sell**: Remove a specified quantity of a stock from the portfolio at a given price. Ensure that the stock exists in the portfolio and that there are enough shares to sell.
3. **Get Portfolio**: Retrieve a copy of the current portfolio.
4. **Get Total Value**: Calculate the total value of the portfolio based on the current stock prices provided.

Your implementation should not use any external libraries. The simulator should handle exceptions appropriately, such as when attempting to sell stocks that do not exist in the portfolio or when a stock price is not provided.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- **Input**: 
  - Buy 10 shares of "AAPL" at $150.
  - Buy 5 shares of "GOOGL" at $2800.
  - Sell 3 shares of "AAPL" at $160.
  - Get Portfolio.
  - Get Total Value with prices {"AAPL": 160, "GOOGL": 2850}.

- **Output**:
  - Portfolio: {'AAPL': 7, 'GOOGL': 5}
  - Total Value: 16700.0

```python
class StockTradingSimulator:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize the portfolio dictionary to store stock symbols and quantities."""
        self.portfolio = {}

    def buy(self, stock_symbol: str, quantity: int, price: float):
        """Add a specified quantity of a stock to the portfolio at a given price.
        
        :param stock_symbol: The symbol of the stock to buy.
        :param quantity: The number of shares to buy.
        :param price: The price per share.
        """
        if stock_symbol in self.portfolio:
            self.portfolio[stock_symbol] += quantity
        else:
            self.portfolio[stock_symbol] = quantity

    def sell(self, stock_symbol: str, quantity: int, price: float):
        """Remove a specified quantity of a stock from the portfolio at a given price.
        
        :param stock_symbol: The symbol of the stock to sell.
        :param quantity: The number of shares to sell.
        :param price: The price per share.
        :raises ValueError: If there are not enough shares to sell.
        :raises KeyError: If the stock does not exist in the portfolio.
        """
        if stock_symbol in self.portfolio:
            if self.portfolio[stock_symbol] >= quantity:
                self.portfolio[stock_symbol] -= quantity
                if self.portfolio[stock_symbol] == 0:
                    del self.portfolio[stock_symbol]
            else:
                raise ValueError(f"Not enough {stock_symbol} to sell.")
        else:
            raise KeyError(f"No {stock_symbol} in portfolio to sell.")

    def get_portfolio(self):
        """Retrieve a copy of the current portfolio.
        
        :return: A dictionary representing the current portfolio.
        """
        return self.portfolio.copy()

    def get_total_value(self, prices: dict):
        """Calculate the total value of the portfolio based on the current stock prices.
        
        :param prices: A dictionary mapping stock symbols to their current prices.
        :return: The total value of the portfolio.
        :raises KeyError: If a stock price is not provided.
        """
        total_value = 0.0
        for stock_symbol, quantity in self.portfolio.items():
            if stock_symbol in prices:
                total_value += quantity * prices[stock_symbol]
            else:
                raise KeyError(f"No current price for {stock_symbol} supplied.")
        return total_value
```<#sep#>). However, floor(t) = 0 in [0,1), so the term for n=0 is 0. Therefore, the sum starts from n=1:

I = sum_{n=1}^∞ integral_{n}^{n+1} [n/(t(1 + t^2))] dt.

Therefore, I = sum_{n=1}^∞ n integral_{n}^{n+1} [1/(t(1 + t^2))] dt.

Now, let's compute the integral inside the sum: integral of 1/(t(1 + t^2)) dt.

We can use partial fractions. Let me recall that 1/(t(1 + t^2)) can be written as A/t + (Bt + C)/(1 + t^2). Let's find A, B, C.

1 = A(1 + t^2) + (Bt + C)t

Expanding:

1 = A + A t^2 + B t^2 + C t

Grouping terms:

1 = A + (A + B) t^2 + C t.

Therefore, coefficients:

A = 1

A + B = 0 ⇒ B = -1

C = 0

Therefore, 1/(t(1 + t^2)) = 1/t - t/(1 + t^2).

So, integral [1/(t(1 + t^2))] dt = integral (1/t - t/(1 + t^2)) dt = ln |t| - (1/2) ln(1 + t^2) + C.

Therefore, the integral from n to n+1 is [ln t - (1/2) ln(1 + t^2)] evaluated from n to n+1.

So, substituting limits:

[ln(n+1) - (1/2) ln(1 + (n+1)^2)] - [ln n - (1/2) ln(1 + n^2)].

Therefore, this simplifies to:

ln((n+1)/n) - (1/2) ln((1 + (n+1)^2)/(1 + n^2)).

So, putting this back into the sum for I:

I = sum_{n=1}^∞ n [ ln((n+1)/n) - (1/2) ln((1 + (n+1)^2)/(1 + n^2)) ]

Wait a minute, this is exactly the same S that we had earlier! So, indeed, S is equal to I, and so our original integral is π/2 - I.

Therefore, the problem reduces to evaluating the sum S = sum_{n=1}^∞ n [ ln((n+1)/n) - (1/2) ln((1 + (n+1)^2)/(1 + n^2)) ]

So, let's see if we can compute this sum S.

Note that S can be split into two sums:

S = sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln((n+1)/n) - (1/2) sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln((1 + (n+1)^2)/(1 + n^2)).

Let’s denote S1 = sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln((n+1)/n) and S2 = sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln((1 + (n+1)^2)/(1 + n^2))

So, S = S1 - (1/2) S2

Let’s try to compute S1 and S2.

Starting with S1:

S1 = sum_{n=1}^∞ n [ln(n+1) - ln n]

As we saw before, this is a divergent series. But perhaps in combination with S2, which may also diverge, they cancel out appropriately.

Similarly, for S2:

S2 = sum_{n=1}^∞ n [ln(1 + (n+1)^2) - ln(1 + n^2)]

This is similar to a telescoping series but multiplied by n.

Alternatively, perhaps we can manipulate S1 and S2 together. Let's see.

Let me try to write S1 and S2.

First, S1:

S1 = sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln(n+1) - sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln n

Similarly, S2 = sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln(1 + (n+1)^2) - sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln(1 + n^2)

Let me consider shifting the index in some of these sums.

For example, let’s denote m = n + 1 in the first sum of S1:

sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln(n+1) = sum_{m=2}^∞ (m - 1) ln m

Similarly, the second sum in S1 is sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln n

Therefore, S1 = sum_{m=2}^∞ (m - 1) ln m - sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln n = sum_{m=2}^∞ (m - 1) ln m - sum_{m=1}^∞ m ln m

Breaking the second sum into m=1 term and the rest:

= [sum_{m=2}^∞ (m - 1) ln m] - [1 ln 1 + sum_{m=2}^∞ m ln m]

Since ln 1 = 0:

= sum_{m=2}^∞ (m - 1 - m) ln m = sum_{m=2}^∞ (- ln m) = - sum_{m=2}^∞ ln m

But this sum is - [sum_{m=1}^∞ ln m - ln 1] = - sum_{m=1}^∞ ln m, since ln 1 = 0. But sum_{m=1}^∞ ln m diverges to infinity, which confirms that S1 is divergent. However, perhaps when combined with S2, things cancel.

Similarly, let's look at S2:

S2 = sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln(1 + (n+1)^2) - sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln(1 + n^2)

Similarly, shift index in the first sum: let m = n + 1, so n = m - 1. Then the first sum becomes:

sum_{m=2}^∞ (m - 1) ln(1 + m^2)

The second sum is sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln(1 + n^2)

Therefore, S2 = sum_{m=2}^∞ (m - 1) ln(1 + m^2) - sum_{n=1}^∞ n ln(1 + n^2)

Breaking the second sum into m=1 term and m>=2:

= sum_{m=2}^∞ (m - 1) ln(1 + m^2) - [1 ln(1 + 1^2) + sum_{m=2}^∞ m ln(1 + m^2)]

= sum_{m=2}^∞ (m - 1) ln(1 + m^2) - ln 2 - sum_{m=2}^∞ m ln(1 + m^2)

= - ln 2 - sum_{m=2}^∞ [m - (m -1)] ln(1 + m^2)

= - ln 2 - sum_{m=2}^∞ ln(1 + m^2)

So, S2 = - ln 2 - sum_{m=2}^∞ ln(1 + m^2)

But sum_{m=2}^∞ ln(1 + m^2) also diverges, so S2 is divergent. Therefore, S1 and S2 are both divergent, but their combination S = S1 - (1/2) S2 might be convergent.

But how?

Let me try to write S:

<#sep#>You are given two English texts and an integer `n`. Your task is to implement a function `get_common_words(text1: str, text2: str, n: int) -> List[str]` that identifies the `n` most common words shared between the two texts, excluding a predefined list of common stop words. The function should return these words in descending order of their combined frequency across both texts.

**Input:**
- `text1`: A string representing the first text.
- `text2`: A string representing the second text.
- `n`: An integer representing the number of common words to return.

**Output:**
- A list of `n` strings, each representing a common word between the two texts, sorted in descending order of their combined frequency.

**Constraints:**
- The input texts are in English.
- The function should ignore common stop words such as "the", "and", "is", etc.
- Assume that the input texts do not contain any special characters other than punctuation marks.

**Sample Input:**
```python
text1 = "The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog."
text2 = "The lazy dog is not amused by the quick brown fox."
n = 2
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
['the', 'quick']
```

```python
from collections import Counter
from typing import List

def get_common_words(text1: str, text2: str, n: int) -> List[str]:
    """
    Returns the n most common words shared between two given texts, excluding common stop words.
    
    :param text1: A string representing the first text.
    :param text2: A string representing the second text.
    :param n: An integer representing the number of common words to return.
    :return: A list of n strings, each representing a common word between the two texts, sorted in descending order of their combined frequency.
    """
    # Define a set of common stop words.
    stop_words = set([
        "the", "and", "is", "in", "it", "to", "of", "a", "that", "i", "for", "on", "with", "as", "you", 
        "he", "she", "at", "by", "this", "from", "or", "which", "one", "an", "be", "have", "has", "had", 
        "but", "not", "so", "if", "then", "when", "where", "why", "how", "all", "any", "both", "each", 
        "few", "more", "most", "other", "some", "such", "no", "nor", "not", "only", "own", "same", "than",
        "too", "very", "s", "t", "can", "will", "just", "don", "should", "now"
    ])
    
    def clean_text(text: str) -> List[str]:
        # Lowercase the text and split it into words.
        words = text.lower().split()
        # Remove punctuation and stop words.
        words = [word.strip(".,!?;:'\"()[]{}") for word in words if word.lower() not in stop_words]
        return words
    
    # Clean both texts.
    words1 = clean_text(text1)
    words2 = clean_text(text2)
    
    # Count the frequency of each word in both texts.
    freq1 = Counter(words1)
    freq2 = Counter(words2)
    
    # Find the common words in both texts.
    common_words = freq1.keys() & freq2.keys()
    
    # Sort the common words by frequency in descending order.
    common_words_by_freq = sorted(common_words, key=lambda w: (freq1[w] + freq2[w]), reverse=True)
    
    # Return the top n words.
    return common_words_by_freq[:n]

<#sep#>You are given a list of dictionaries, each representing a book with keys for the book's title, author, publication year, and genre. Your task is to sort this list of books first by their publication year in ascending order. If two books share the same publication year, they should be sorted by their title in ascending order. Implement a function `sort_books_by_year_and_title` that takes a list of books and returns the sorted list.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[
    {"title": "The Great Gatsby", "author": "F. Scott Fitzgerald", "publication_year": 1925, "genre": "Fiction"},
    {"title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee", "publication_year": 1960, "genre": "Fiction"},

    {"title": "The Catcher in the Rye", "author": "J.D. Salinger", "publication_year": 1951, "genre": "Fiction"},
    {"title": "Animal Farm", "author": "George Orwell", "publication_year": 1945, "genre": "Fiction"}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[
    {"title": "The Great Gatsby", "author": "F. Scott Fitzgerald", "publication_year": 1925, "genre": "Fiction"},
    {"title": "Brave New World", "author": "Aldous Huxley", "publication_year": 1932, "genre": "Dystopian"},
    {"title": "Animal Farm", "author": "George Orwell", "publication_year": 1945, "genre": "Fiction"},
    {"title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "publication_year": 1949, "genre": "Dystopian"},
    {"title": "The Catcher in the Rye", "author": "J.D. Salinger", "publication_year": 1951, "genre": "Fiction"},
    {"title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee", "publication_year": 1960, "genre": "Fiction"}
]
```

```python
def sort_books_by_year_and_title(books):
    """
    Sorts a list of books by their publication year in ascending order.
    If two books have the same publication year, they are sorted by their title in ascending order.

    :param books: List of dictionaries, each representing a book with keys 'title', 'author', 'publication_year', and 'genre'.
    :return: Sorted list of books.
    """
    return sorted(books, key=lambda book: (book['publication_year'], book['title']))

# Example usage
books = [
    {"title": "The Great Gatsby", "author": "F. Scott Fitzgerald", "publication_year": 1925, "genre": "Fiction"},
    {"title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee", "publication_year": 1960, "genre": "Fiction"},
    {"title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "publication_year": 1949, "genre": "Dystopian"},
    {"title": "Brave New World", "author": "Aldous Huxley", "publication_year": 1932, "genre": "Dystopian"},
    {"title": "The Catcher in the Rye", "author": "J.D. Salinger", "publication_year": 1951, "genre": "Fiction"},
    {"title": "Animal Farm", "author": "George Orwell", "publication_year": 1945, "genre": "Fiction"}
]

sorted_books = sort_books_by_year_and_title(books)
print(sorted_books)

integral_{1/n}^{1/(n+1)} [1/( (1/u)(1 + (1/u)^2 )) ] * (-1/u^2) du

1/( (1/u)(1 + 1/u^2 )) = u / (1 + 1/u^2) = u^3 / (u^2 + 1)

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Triangle` class that models a triangle in a 2D plane. The class should support the following functionalities:
- Calculate the area of the triangle.
- Calculate the perimeter of the triangle.
- Determine if the triangle is a right triangle.

The `__init__` method should accept three tuples as arguments, each representing the coordinates of a vertex of the triangle. For instance, `Triangle((0, 0), (3, 0), (0, 4))` should create a right triangle with vertices at (0, 0), (3, 0), and (0, 4).

**Sample Input:**
```python
triangle = Triangle((0, 0), (3, 0), (0, 4))
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
print(triangle.area())  # Output: 6.0
print(triangle.perimeter())  # Output: 12.0
print(triangle.is_right_triangle())  # Output: True
```

```python
import math

class Triangle:
    def __init__(self, vertex1, vertex2, vertex3):
        """
        Initialize a Triangle object with three vertices.
        
        :param vertex1: A tuple representing the coordinates of the first vertex.
        :param vertex2: A tuple representing the coordinates of the second vertex.
        :param vertex3: A tuple representing the coordinates of the third vertex.
        """
        self.v1 = vertex1
        self.v2 = vertex2
        self.v3 = vertex3

    def distance(self, point1, point2):
        """
        Calculate the Euclidean distance between two points.
        
        :param point1: A tuple representing the coordinates of the first point.
        :param point2: A tuple representing the coordinates of the second point.
        :return: The Euclidean distance between point1 and point2.
        """
        return math.sqrt((point1[0] - point2[0]) ** 2 + (point1[1] - point2[1]) ** 2)

    def area(self):
        """
        Calculate the area of the triangle using the Shoelace formula.
        
        :return: The area of the triangle.
        """
        x1, y1 = self.v1
        x2, y2 = self.v2
        x3, y3 = self.v3
        return abs((x1*(y2 - y3) + x2*(y3 - y1) + x3*(y1 - y2)) / 2.0)

    def perimeter(self):
        """
        Calculate the perimeter of the triangle.
        
        :return: The perimeter of the triangle.
        """
        return self.distance(self.v1, self.v2) + self.distance(self.v2, self.v3) + self.distance(self.v3, self.v1)

    def is_right_triangle(self):
        """
        Determine if the triangle is a right triangle.
        
        :return: True if the triangle is a right triangle, False otherwise.
        """
        a_squared = self.distance(self.v1, self.v2) ** 2
        b_squared = self.distance(self.v2, self.v3) ** 2
        c_squared = self.distance(self.v3, self.v1) ** 2
        return (math.isclose(a_squared + b_squared, c_squared) or
                math.isclose(a_squared + c_squared, b_squared) or
                math.isclose(b_squared + c_squared, a_squared))
```<#sep#>), floor(t) is n for t in [n, n+1). So, the integral from 1 to ∞ is sum_{n=1}^∞ n integral_{n}^{n+1} 1/(t(1 + t^2)) dt.

Therefore, the integral I = sum_{n=1}^\infty n integral_{n}^{n+1} 1/(t(1 + t^2)) dt.

But each integral can be written as:

integral_{n}^{n+1} 1/(t(1 + t^2)) dt = (1/2) [ ln(1 + n^2) - 2 ln n - ln(1 + (n+1)^2) + 2 ln(n+1) ]

= (1/2) [ (ln(1 + n^2) - ln(1 + (n+1)^2)) + 2 (ln(n+1) - ln n) ]

So, multiplying by n:

n * integral_{n}^{n+1} 1/(t(1 + t^2)) dt = (n/2) [ ln( (1 + n^2)/(1 + (n+1)^2) ) + 2 ln( (n+1)/n ) ]

= (n/2) ln( (1 + n^2)/(1 + (n+1)^2) ) + n ln( (n+1)/n )

Which is exactly the term we have in S.

Therefore, I = sum_{n=1}^\infty [ n ln((n+1)/n) - (n/2) ln( (1 + (n+1)^2)/(1 + n^2) ) ]

But this is the same S as before, so we’re back where we started.

Given that I can't see a telescoping or another method to compute S, perhaps it's necessary to look for patterns or known series.

Wait, another thought. Let me consider the term ln((n+1)/n) and ln((1 + (n+1)^2)/(1 + n^2)).

Let me compute the sum S again:

S = sum_{n=1}^\infty [n ln((n+1)/n) - (n/2) ln((1 + (n+1)^2)/(1 + n^2))]

Perhaps we can relate this to the logarithm of a product. Let's recall that sum ln(a_n) = ln( product a_n )

But here, we have terms like n ln(b_n) and n ln(c_n). Not sure.

Alternatively, let's consider the product representation.

For the first part, sum_{n=1}^\infty n ln((n+1)/n) = sum_{n=1}^\infty n [ln(n+1) - ln n]

But we can write this as:

sum_{n=1}^\infty n ln(n+1) - sum_{n=1}^\infty n ln n

Let me shift the index in the first sum:

sum_{n=1}^\infty n ln(n+1) = sum_{m=2}^\infty (m - 1) ln m

So, S1 = sum_{m=2}^\infty (m - 1) ln m - sum_{m=1}^\infty m ln m

= sum_{m=2}^\infty (m - 1) ln m - sum_{m=2}^\infty m ln m - 1*ln 1

= sum_{m=2}^\infty [ (m - 1) ln m - m ln m ] - 0

= - sum_{m=2}^\infty ln m

Which diverges to -∞.

Similarly, the sum S2:

S2 = sum_{n=1}^\infty n [ln(1 + (n+1)^2) - ln(1 + n^2)]

= sum_{n=1}^\infty n ln(1 + (n+1)^2) - sum_{n=1}^\infty n ln(1 + n^2)

Again, shift the index in the first sum:

= sum_{m=2}^\infty (m - 1) ln(1 + m^2) - sum_{m=1}^\infty m ln(1 + m^2)

= sum_{m=2}^\infty (m - 1 - m) ln(1 + m^2) - 1*ln(1 + 1^2)

= - sum_{m=2}^\infty ln(1 + m^2) - ln 2

Also diverges to -∞.

But in the combination S = S1 - (1/2) S2, we have:

S = (- sum_{m=2}^\infty ln m) - (1/2)( - sum_{m=2}^\infty ln(1 + m^2) - ln 2 )

= - sum_{m=2}^\infty ln m + (1/2) sum_{m=2}^\infty ln(1 + m^2) + (1/2) ln 2

This can be written as:

= (1/2) ln 2 + sum_{m=2}^\infty [ - ln m + (1/2) ln(1 + m^2) ]

= (1/2) ln 2 + sum_{m=2}^\infty ln [ (1 + m^2)^{1/2} / m ]

= (1/2) ln 2 + sum_{m=2}^\infty ln [ sqrt(1 + m^2)/m ]

= (1/2) ln 2 + sum_{m=2}^\infty ln sqrt(1 + 1/m^2 )

= (1/2) ln 2 + sum_{m=2}^\infty (1/2) ln(1 + 1/m^2 )

= (1/2) [ ln 2 + sum_{m=2}^\infty ln(1 + 1/m^2 ) ]

Now, this sum is the logarithm of a product:

sum_{m=2}^\infty ln(1 + 1/m^2 ) = ln( product_{m=2}^\infty (1 + 1/m^2) )

The product product_{m=1}^\infty (1 + 1/m^2) is known. Let’s recall that:

sinh(π)/(π) = product_{m=1}^\infty (1 + 1/m^2 )

But I need to verify this. Let me recall that the infinite product for sinh(π z) is:

sinh(π z) = π z product_{n=1}^\infty (1 + z^2/n^2 )

For z=1, sinh(π) = π product_{n=1}^\infty (1 + 1/n^2 )

Therefore, product_{n=1}^\infty (1 + 1/n^2 ) = sinh(π)/π.

Therefore, product_{m=2}^\infty (1 + 1/m^2 ) = sinh(π)/(π(1 + 1/1^2 )) = sinh(π)/(π * 2)

Therefore, sum_{m=2}^\infty ln(1 + 1/m^2 ) = ln( sinh(π)/(2π) )

So, coming back:

S = (1/2) [ ln 2 + ln( sinh(π)/(2π) ) ]

= (1/2) [ ln 2 + ln(sinh(π)) - ln(2π) ]

= (1/2) [ ln(sinh(π)) - ln(π) ]

= (1/2) ln( sinh(π)/π )

But sinh(π) = (e^π - e^{-π})/2

So,

sinh(π)/π = (e^π - e^{-π})/(2π)

Thus, S = (1/2) ln( (e^π - e^{-π})/(2π) )

But how does this relate to the original integral?

Original integral is π/2 - S, so:

Integral = π/2 - (1/2) ln( (e^π - e^{-π})/(2π) )

Simplify this expression:

= π/2 - (1/2) [ ln(e^π - e^{-π}) - ln(2π) ]

= π/2 - (1/2) ln(e^π - e^{-π}) + (1/2) ln(2π)

             result["Certificate"]["ImportedAt"] = datetime_to_epoch(self.created_at)

<#sep#>You are given a list of `n` points in a 2D plane, where each point is represented as a tuple `(x, y)`. Your task is to find the pair of points that are closest to each other in terms of Euclidean distance. Implement a function `find_minimal_distance_pair(points)` that returns a tuple containing the coordinates of the two closest points. Your solution should have a time complexity better than O(N^2).

**Constraints:**
- 2 ≤ n ≤ 10^5
- -10^9 ≤ x, y ≤ 10^9

**Sample Input:**
```python
points = [(2, 3), (12, 30), (40, 50), (5, 1), (12, 10), (3, 4)]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
((2, 3), (3, 4))
```

```python
import math

def distance(p1, p2):
    """Calculate the Euclidean distance between two points p1 and p2."""
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0]) ** 2 + (p1[1] - p2[1]) ** 2)

def closest_pair_naive(points):
    """Find the closest pair of points using a naive O(N^2) approach."""
    min_dist = float('inf')
    pair = None
    n = len(points)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            d = distance(points[i], points[j])
            if d < min_dist:
                min_dist = d
                pair = (points[i], points[j])
    return pair, min_dist

def closest_split_pair(strip, d, closest_pair):
    """Find the closest pair of points that lie across the dividing line."""
    min_dist = d
    strip = sorted(strip, key=lambda point: point[1])
    n = len(strip)
    for i in range(n):
        j = i + 1
        while j < n and strip[j][1] - strip[i][1] < min_dist:
            d = distance(strip[i], strip[j])
            if d < min_dist:
                min_dist = d
                closest_pair = strip[i], strip[j]
            j += 1
    return closest_pair, min_dist

def closest_pair_rec(px, py):
    """Recursively find the closest pair of points using a divide and conquer approach."""
    if len(px) <= 3:
        return closest_pair_naive(px)
    mid = len(px) // 2
    Qx = px[:mid]
    Rx = px[mid:]
    midpoint = px[mid][0]
    Qy = list(filter(lambda x: x[0] <= midpoint, py))
    Ry = list(filter(lambda x: x[0] > midpoint, py))
    (p1, q1), dist1 = closest_pair_rec(Qx, Qy)
    (p2, q2), dist2 = closest_pair_rec(Rx, Ry)
    delta = min(dist1, dist2)
    closest_pair = (p1, q1) if dist1 <= dist2 else (p2, q2)
    strip = [point for point in py if abs(point[0] - midpoint) < delta]
    closest_split, dist_split = closest_split_pair(strip, delta, closest_pair)
    return closest_split, dist_split if dist_split < delta else closest_pair, delta

def find_minimal_distance_pair(points):
    """Find the pair of points with the minimal distance between them."""
    px = sorted(points, key=lambda x: x[0])
    py = sorted(points, key=lambda x: x[1])
    (p1, p2), _ = closest_pair_rec(px, py)
    return (p1, p2)

So the entire expression's length<#sep#>Upcoming Compression changes in Boto3
Hi there,

I'm reaching out from the Boto3 team about a recently released feature (https://github.com/boto/botocore/pull/2959) that may have some implications on how moto works with request body parsing. In order to help optimize bytes sent over the wire, boto3 will slowly start onboarding some services and operations to compress their request payload by default (only gzip is supported currently). This should improve latency for particularly large calls with the end goal of streamlining throughput and performance for customers.

With this onboarding, we'll be updating our service models by adding new traits per operation where we notice particularly large request sizes. Doing some initial testing with moto, it looks like the class setup in [moto.core.response.py](https://github.com/getmoto/moto/blob/master/moto/core/responses.py#L264-L296) is making some assumptions about the body that may cause issues as this is rolled out. Namely that any bytes body is assumed to be a utf-8 string which won't be true for a gzip file.

## Possible Solution(s)

### Decompress bodies before processing
For our implementation we utilize the standard method of `Content-Encoding` headers ([RFC 9110 8.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#name-content-encoding)) in our requests to denote a compressed body. This will likely be the most effective method to recognize these changes to request payloads in moto and decompress before doing your body processing workflow.

This should be safe to implement uniformly across all AWS services _except_ s3 which has some specific implications around `Content-Encoding` in operations like `PutObject` where the body should be left untouched. For whatever solution is implemented, we'd recommend exempting S3 from this as they're considered out of scope for this feature.

### Disable Request Compression in moto mock
I'm not immediately sure about the feasibility on this option, but we do provide an escape hatch in boto3 to explicitly disable this. Both an environment variable (`AWS_DISABLE_REQUEST_COMPRESSION`) and a Config option (`disable_request_compression`) can be used to shut off this functionality. It may be possible for moto to enable this when the mock is used to ensure the body will never be compressed.

## Next Steps
We don't have firm dates on when we'll start rolling this out to services, but I can share the we're intending to start with some operations in CloudWatch. We wanted to proactively reach out to hopefully avoid the friction moto users had with the recent SQS changes that required (https://github.com/getmoto/moto/pull/6331).

I'm happy to talk through any implementation specifics or provide more information that might be needed to align on a fix in moto. I couldn't find anything generalized in the moto codebase for handling this currently so I'm assuming it will require some net-new code in the BaseResponse class unless there's a better place to handle this.

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class `CSVFileHandler` that provides functionalities to read, write, and append data to CSV files. The class should handle exceptions such as file not found errors and invalid CSV formats gracefully. The `read_csv` method should return a list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a row in the CSV file. The `write_csv` method should overwrite the existing file with new data, and the `append_to_csv` method should add new data to the end of the file. Your implementation should include appropriate comments and docstrings.

**Sample Input:**
```
# File: example.csv
name,age
John,30
Jane,25

# Operations:
handler = CSVFileHandler()
data = handler.read_csv('example.csv')
handler.write_csv('example.csv', [{'name': 'Alice', 'age': 28}, {'name': 'Bob', 'age': 24}])
handler.append_to_csv('example.csv', [{'name': 'Charlie', 'age': 22}])
```

**Sample Output:**
```
# File: example.csv after operations
name,age
Alice,28
Bob,24
Charlie,22
```

```python
import csv
from typing import List, Dict


class CSVFileHandler:
    """A class to handle reading, writing, and appending to CSV files."""

    def read_csv(self, file_path: str) -> List[Dict]:
        """
        Reads a CSV file and returns a list of dictionaries where each
        dictionary represents a row in the CSV file.

        :param file_path: Path to the CSV file.
        :return: List of dictionaries where each dictionary is a row.
        :raises FileNotFoundError: If the file is not found.
        :raises csv.Error: If the CSV file has an invalid format.
        """
        try:
            with open(file_path, mode='r', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as file:
                reader = csv.DictReader(file)
                return list(reader)
        except FileNotFoundError:
            print(f"Error: The file at {file_path} was not found.")
            raise
        except csv.Error as e:
            print(f"Error reading CSV file at {file_path}: {e}")
            raise

    def write_csv(self, file_path: str, data: List[Dict]):
        """
        Writes a list of dictionaries to a CSV file, overwriting any existing data.

        :param file_path: Path to the CSV file.
        :param data: List of dictionaries to write to the file.
        :raises csv.Error: If the CSV file cannot be written properly.
        """
        if not data:
            print("Warning: No data to write to the CSV file.")
            return

        try:
            fieldnames = data[0].keys()
            with open(file_path, mode='w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as file:
                writer = csv.DictWriter(file, fieldnames=fieldnames)
                writer.writeheader()
                writer.writerows(data)
        except csv.Error as e:
            print(f"Error writing to CSV file at {file_path}: {e}")
            raise
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Unexpected error occurred: {e}")
            raise

    def append_to_csv(self, file_path: str, data: List[Dict]):
        """
        Appends a list of dictionaries to a CSV file.

        :param file_path: Path to the CSV file.
        :param data: List of dictionaries to append to the file.
        :raises csv.Error: If the CSV file cannot be appended properly.
        """
        if not data:
            print("Warning: No data to append to the CSV file.")
            return

        try:
            if not self._file_exists(file_path):
                raise FileNotFoundError

            fieldnames = data[0].keys()
            with open(file_path, mode='a', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as file:
                writer = csv.DictWriter(file, fieldnames=fieldnames)
                writer.writerows(data)
        except FileNotFoundError:
            print(f"Error: The file at {file_path} was not found.")
            raise
        except csv.Error as e:
            print(f"Error appending to CSV file at {file_path}: {e}")
            raise
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Unexpected error occurred: {e}")
            raise

    @staticmethod
    def _file_exists(file_path: str) -> bool:
        """
        Checks if a file exists at the given path.

        :param file_path: Path to the file.
        :return: True if the file exists, False otherwise.
        """
        try:
            with open(file_path, mode='r', encoding='utf-8'):
                return True
        except FileNotFoundError:
            return False
```<#sep#> sinh(π) can be expressed in terms of e^π and e^{-π}:

sinh(π) = (e^π - e^{-π}) / 2

Therefore,

ln(sinh(π)/π) = ln((e^π - e^{-π}) / (2π))

Thus, the integral is:

π/2 - (1/2) ln( (e^π - e^{-π}) / (2π) )

Perhaps simplifying further:

= π/2 - (1/2) [ ln(e^π - e^{-π}) - ln(2π) ]

= π/2 - (1/2) ln(e^π - e^{-π}) + (1/2) ln(2π)

But I don't think this simplifies further in terms of elementary constants.

Alternatively, considering that the problem might have a solution in terms of Catalan's constant or other known constants, but I’m not sure. Alternatively, perhaps there's a mistake in the process, and the actual answer is π/2 - 1, but I need to verify.

Alternatively, let's check with a numerical approximation:

The integral from 0 to π/2 of {tan x}/tan x dx = π/2 - I

Where I = (1/2) ln( sinh(π)/π )

Numerically, sinh(π) ≈ 11.54873935, as before. Then sinh(π)/π ≈ 3.67608, ln(3.67608) ≈ 1.302, so I ≈ 0.651. Then, π/2 ≈ 1.5708, so the integral ≈ 1.5708 - 0.651 ≈ 0.9198.

But if I numerically integrate the original function, what would I get?

Let's approximate the integral from 0 to π/2 of {tan x}/tan x dx.

From x=0 to x=π/4, tan x is between 0 and 1, so {tan x}/tan x = 1. So, integral from 0 to π/4 is π/4 ≈ 0.7854.

From π/4 to π/2, tan x is from 1 to ∞. So, {tan x} = tan x - n when tan x is in [n, n+1).

Therefore, for x in [arctan(n), arctan(n+1)), {tan x} = tan x - n.

Thus, {tan x}/tan x = 1 - n / tan x.

Therefore, the integral from π/4 to π/2 is sum_{n=1}^\infty integral_{arctan(n)}^{arctan(n+1)} [1 - n/tan x] dx

Let's compute this sum numerically up to a few terms to approximate:

First term, n=1: integral from arctan(1)=π/4 to arctan(2)≈1.10715 of [1 - 1/tan x] dx.

Compute this integral:

Integral of 1 dx from π/4 to arctan(2): arctan(2) - π/4 ≈1.10715 - 0.7854 ≈0.32175

Integral of -1/tan x dx from π/4 to arctan(2): - integral of cot x dx = - [ln sin x] from π/4 to arctan(2)

sin(arctan(2)) = 2/sqrt(5), so ln(sin(arctan(2))) = ln(2/sqrt(5)) = ln2 - (1/2)ln5 ≈0.6931 - 0.8047 ≈-0.1116

Similarly, sin(π/4)=sqrt(2)/2, so ln(sqrt(2)/2) = (1/2)ln2 - ln2 = - (1/2)ln2 ≈-0.3466

Thus, the integral of -cot x from π/4 to arctan(2) is - [ (-0.1116) - (-0.3466) ] = - (0.235) ≈-0.235

Therefore, the integral for n=1 is 0.32175 -0.235 ≈0.08675

Next term, n=2: integral from arctan(2)≈1.10715 to arctan(3)≈1.2490 of [1 - 2/tan x] dx.

Integral of 1 dx: arctan(3) - arctan(2)≈1.2490 - 1.10715≈0.14185

Integral of -2/tan x dx: -2 integral cot x dx = -2 [ln sin x] from 1.10715 to 1.2490

sin(arctan(3)) = 3/sqrt(10), ln(3/sqrt(10))=ln3 - (1/2)ln10≈1.0986 - 1.1513≈-0.0527

sin(arctan(2))=2/sqrt(5), ln(2/sqrt(5))≈-0.1116

So, the integral is -2 [ (-0.0527) - (-0.1116) ] = -2 (0.0589)≈-0.1178

Thus, integral for n=2: 0.14185 -0.1178≈0.02405

Next, n=3: integral from arctan(3)≈1.2490 to arctan(4)≈1.3258 of [1 - 3/tan x] dx

Integral of 1 dx: 1.3258 -1.2490≈0.0768

Integral of -3/tan x dx: -3 [ln sin(1.3258) - ln sin(1.2490)]

Compute sin(arctan(4))=4/sqrt(17), ln(4/sqrt(17))=ln4 - (1/2)ln17≈1.3863 - 1.4708≈-0.0845

sin(arctan(3))=3/sqrt(10), ln(3/sqrt(10))≈-0.0527

So, the integral is -3 [ (-0.0845) - (-0.0527) ] = -3 (-0.0318)≈0.0954

Thus, integral for n=3: 0.0768 +0.0954≈0.1722

Wait, that can't be. Wait, the integrand for n=3 is 1 -3/tan x. So, if tan x is between 3 and 4, tan x ≈3 to4, so cot x =1/tan x between 1/4 and 1/3. So, 3/tan x is between 3/4 and 1. Therefore, 1 -3/tan x is between 0 and 1/4. Therefore, the integrand is positive but less than 1/4, and the interval is about 0.0768 in length, so the integral should be around (average value) 0.1 * 0.0768 ≈0.00768. But my calculation gave 0.0768 +0.0954≈0.1722, which is incorrect. I must have made a mistake in the sign.

Wait, the integral for n=3 is:

integral [1 -3/tan x] dx = integral 1 dx -3 integral cot x dx

Integral 1 dx is 0.0768.

Integral cot x dx from arctan(3) to arctan(4):

= [ln sin x] from arctan(3) to arctan(4)

= ln(4/sqrt(17)) - ln(3/sqrt(10)) = ln(4/3) + (1/2)(ln10 - ln17) ≈0.2877 + (1/2)(2.3026 -2.8332)≈0.2877 -0.2653≈0.0224

Therefore, -3 * this integral is -3 *0.0224≈-0.0672

Therefore, the total integral for n=3 is 0.0768 -0.0672≈0.0096

<#sep#>You are given a text file named `input.txt` containing a body of text. Your task is to write a Python script that processes this text to count the frequency of each word, excluding common stop words. The stop words are provided in a predefined list. The script should be case-insensitive and should remove punctuation. The output should be written to a new file named `word_count_output.txt` with each line containing a word and its frequency, sorted in descending order of frequency.

**Sample Input:**
```
The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. The dog was not amused.
```

**Sample Output:**
```
dog 2
the 2
quick 1
brown 1
fox 1
jumps 1
over 1
lazy 1
was 1
not 1
amused 1
```

```python
import string
from collections import Counter

# Predefined list of stop words
stop_words = set(['the', 'be', 'to', 'of', 'and', 'a', 'in', 'that', 'have', 'i', 'it', 'for', 'not', 'on', 'with', 'he', 'as', 'you', 'do', 'at', 'this', 'but', 'his', 'by', 'from', 'they', 'we', 'say', 'her', 'she', 'or', 'an', 'will', 'my', 'one', 'all', 'would', 'there', 'their', 'what', 'so', 'up', 'out', 'if', 'about', 'who', 'get', 'which', 'go', 'me', 'when', 'can', 'like', 'time', 'no', 'just', 'him', 'know', 'take', 'people', 'into', 'year', 'your', 'good', 'some', 'could', 'them', 'see', 'other', 'than', 'then', 'now', 'look', 'only', 'come', 'its', 'over', 'think', 'also', 'back', 'after', 'use', 'two', 'how', 'our', 'work', 'first', 'well', 'way', 'even', 'new', 'want', 'because', 'any', 'these', 'give', 'day', 'most', 'us'])

def read_and_filter_file(filename):
    """
    Reads a file, converts text to lowercase, removes punctuation, and filters out stop words.
    
    :param filename: Name of the file to read.
    :return: List of filtered words.
    """
    with open(filename, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
        text = file.read().lower()

    translator = str.maketrans('', '', string.punctuation)
    text_no_punct = text.translate(translator)

    words = text_no_punct.split()
    filtered_words = [word for word in words if word not in stop_words]

    return filtered_words

def word_count(words):
    """
    Counts the frequency of each word in the list.
    
    :param words: List of words.
    :return: Counter object with word frequencies.
    """
    return Counter(words)

def write_word_count(word_count_dict, output_file):
    """
    Writes word frequencies to a file in descending order of frequency.
    
    :param word_count_dict: Counter object with word frequencies.
    :param output_file: Name of the file to write the output.
    """
    with open(output_file, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as file:
        for word, count in word_count_dict.most_common():
            file.write(f'{word} {count}\n')

if __name__ == '__main__':
    input_file = 'input.txt'
    output_file = 'word_count_output.txt'

    words = read_and_filter_file(input_file)
    counted_words = word_count(words)
    write_word_count(counted_words, output_file)
```<#sep#>) + (1/2)(ln10 - ln17) ≈0.2877 + (1/2)(2.3026 -2.8332)≈0.2877 -0.2653≈0.0224

Integral of 1 dx:0.0476

Integral of -4/tan x dx: -4 [ln sin(arctan(5)) - ln sin(arctan(4))]

sin(arctan(4))=4/sqrt(17), ln(4/sqrt(17))≈1.3863 - 1.4708≈-0.0845

Therefore, integral of cot x is -0.0385 - (-0.0845)=0.046

Thus, -4 *0.046≈-0.184, so integral is 0.0476 -0.184≈-0.1364

But this can't be right since the integrand 1 -4/tan x. For x between arctan(4) and arctan(5), tan x is between 4 and 5, so 4/tan x is between 0.8 and 1. Thus, 1 -4/tan x is between 0 and 0.2. So, the integral should be positive. But calculation gives negative, which is a mistake.

Wait, the integral of cot x from arctan(4) to arctan(5) is [ln sin(arctan(5)) - ln sin(arctan(4))] = [ln(5/sqrt(26)) - ln(4/sqrt(17))] = ln(5/4) + (1/2)ln(17/26) ≈0.2231 -0.2412≈-0.0181

Therefore, -4*(-0.0181)≈0.0724

Therefore, integral is 0.0476 +0.0724≈0.12

But that contradicts the expectation. Wait, I think I made another error. Let me recalculate:

sin(arctan(5))=5/sqrt(26)≈5/5.0990≈0.9806

ln(0.9806)= -0.0196

sin(arctan(4))=4/sqrt(17)≈4/4.1231≈0.9701

ln(0.9701)= -0.0304

Thus, difference: -0.0196 - (-0.0304)=0.0108

Multiply by -4: -4*0.0108= -0.0432

Therefore, the integral for n=4 is 0.0476 -0.0432≈0.0044

Therefore, adding this to the previous terms:

n=1:0.08675

n=2:0.02405

n=3:0.0096

n=4:0.0044

Total ≈0.1248

Continuing to n=5:

arctan(5)≈1.3734 to arctan(6)≈1.4056, length≈0.0322

Integral of 1 dx:0.0322

Integral of -5/tan x dx: -5 [ln sin(arctan(6)) - ln sin(arctan(5))]

sin(arctan(6))=6/sqrt(37)≈6/6.0827≈0.9864, ln≈-0.0137

sin(arctan(5))≈5/sqrt(26)≈0.9806, ln≈-0.0196

Difference: -0.0137 - (-0.0196)=0.0059

Multiply by -5: -5*0.0059≈-0.0295

Integral for n=5:0.0322 -0.0295≈0.0027

Total sum now≈0.1248 +0.0027≈0.1275

Continuing:

n=6: interval≈arctan(6) to arctan(7), length≈1.4289 -1.4056≈0.0233

Integral of 1 dx:0.0233

Integral of -6/tan x dx: -6 [ln sin(arctan(7)) - ln sin(arctan(6))]

sin(arctan(7))=7/sqrt(50)=7/7.0711≈0.9899, ln≈-0.0101

sin(arctan(6))≈6/sqrt(37)≈0.9864, ln≈-0.0137

Difference: -0.0101 - (-0.0137)=0.0036

Multiply by -6: -6*0.0036≈-0.0216

Integral for n=6:0.0233 -0.0216≈0.0017

Total sum≈0.1275 +0.0017≈0.1292

Similarly, n=7:

arctan(7) to arctan(8), length≈0.0164

Integral of 1 dx:0.0164

Integral of -7/tan x dx: similar steps, approximate to≈0.001

Total≈0.0164 -0.001≈0.0154 (but this might not be accurate)

Continuing this way, each term contributes less and less. 

Summing the first few terms:

n=1:≈0.087

n=2:≈0.024

n=3:≈0.010

n=4:≈0.004

n=5:≈0.003

n=6:≈0.002

Adding these up: 0.087 +0.024=0.111; +0.010=0.121; +0.004=0.125; +0.003=0.128; +0.002=0.130; higher terms contribute maybe 0.004 more.

So, total integral from π/4 to π/2≈0.134. But the numerical approximation is very rough.

Adding this to the first part, which is π/4≈0.7854, total integral≈0.7854 +0.134≈0.9194, which is close to the previous estimate of ≈0.9198. So, the symbolic answer is π/2 - (1/2) ln(sinh(π)/π )≈0.9198, which matches the numerical approximation.

However, the problem likely expects an exact expression in terms of logarithms and pi. So, the answer is:

π/2 - (1/2) ln(sinh(π)/π )

But sinh(π) can also be written using exponents:

sinh(π) = (e^π - e^{-π})/2

Therefore,

Integral = π/2 - (1/2) ln\left( \frac{e^π - e^{-π}}{2π} \right )

This can be further simplified:

= π/2 - (1/2) [ ln(e^π - e^{-π}) - ln(2π) ]

= π/2 - (1/2) ln(e^π - e^{-π}) + (1/2) ln(2π)

But since this doesn't simplify into elementary terms, I think the answer is best expressed as:

π<#sep#>1 to 280, sum N^l → but in Python, this would be written as

@responses.activate
def test():

        responses.POST,
        url='http://someurl',
        body="4",

            matchers.header_matcher({"Accept": "text/plain"})
        ])

     boto3.client('s3', region_name='us-east-1')
     s3.put_object(Bucket='mybucket', Key="name", Body="value")
     code_under_test()


def code_under_test():
    body = boto3.resource('s3', region_name='us-east-1').Object('mybucket', 'name').get()['Body'].read().decode("utf-8")
    with requests.get("http://someurl") as r:
        pass

<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a RESTful API using Flask that supports CRUD operations on a collection of books. Each book must have the following attributes: `id`, `title`, `author`, and `publication_year`. The data should be stored in an SQLite database named `books.db`. Your API should handle the following requests:

1. **Create a Book**: `POST /books` - Accepts JSON data with `title`, `author`, and `publication_year`. Returns a success message if the book is created successfully.
2. **Read All Books**: `GET /books` - Returns a list of all books in JSON format.
3. **Read a Single Book**: `GET /books/<book_id>` - Returns the details of the book with the specified `book_id` in JSON format.
4. **Update a Book**: `PUT /books/<book_id>` - Accepts JSON data with `title`, `author`, and `publication_year` to update the book with the specified `book_id`. Returns a success message if the book is updated successfully.
5. **Delete a Book**: `DELETE /books/<book_id>` - Deletes the book with the specified `book_id`. Returns a success message if the book is deleted successfully.

**Sample Input/Output:**

- **Create a Book**:
  - Request: `POST /books` with JSON `{"title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "publication_year": 1949}`
  - Response: `{"message": "Book created successfully"}`

- **Read All Books**:
  - Request: `GET /books`
  - Response: `[{"id": 1, "title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "publication_year": 1949}]`

- **Read a Single Book**:
  - Request: `GET /books/1`
  - Response: `{"id": 1, "title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "publication_year": 1949}`

- **Update a Book**:
  - Request: `PUT /books/1` with JSON `{"title": "Animal Farm", "author": "George Orwell", "publication_year": 1945}`
  - Response: `{"message": "Book updated successfully"}`

- **Delete a Book**:
  - Request: `DELETE /books/1`
  - Response: `{"message": "Book deleted successfully"}`

```python
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
import sqlite3

app = Flask(__name__)
DATABASE = 'books.db'

def init_db():
    """
    Initializes the SQLite database with a 'books' table if it does not already exist.
    """
    with sqlite3.connect(DATABASE) as conn:
        c = conn.cursor()
        c.execute('''
            CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS books (
                id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
                title TEXT NOT NULL,
                author TEXT NOT NULL,
                publication_year INTEGER NOT NULL
            )
        ''')
        conn.commit()

def get_db_connection():
    """
    Establishes a connection to the SQLite database and sets the row factory to sqlite3.Row.
    """
    conn = sqlite3.connect(DATABASE)
    conn.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
    return conn

@app.route('/books', methods=['POST'])
def create_book():
    """
    Creates a new book entry in the database based on the JSON data provided in the request.
    """
    new_book = request.get_json()
    title = new_book.get('title')
    author = new_book.get('author')
    publication_year = new_book.get('publication_year')
    
    if not title or not author or not publication_year:
        return jsonify({"error": "title, author, and publication_year are required"}), 400

    conn = get_db_connection()
    conn.execute('INSERT INTO books (title, author, publication_year) VALUES (?, ?, ?)',
                 (title, author, publication_year))
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()
    return jsonify({'message': 'Book created successfully'}), 201

@app.route('/books', methods=['GET'])
def get_books():
    """
    Retrieves all books from the database and returns them as a JSON list.
    """
    conn = get_db_connection()
    books = conn.execute('SELECT * FROM books').fetchall()
    conn.close()
    return jsonify([dict(book) for book in books]), 200

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['GET'])
def get_book(book_id):
    """
    Retrieves a single book from the database based on the provided book_id.
    """
    conn = get_db_connection()
    book = conn.execute('SELECT * FROM books WHERE id = ?', (book_id,)).fetchone()
    conn.close()
    if book is None:
        return jsonify({"message": "Book not found"}), 404
    else:
        return jsonify(dict(book)), 200

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['PUT'])
def update_book(book_id):
    """
    Updates the book with the specified book_id based on the JSON data provided in the request.
    """
    updated_data = request.get_json()
    title = updated_data.get('title')
    author = updated_data.get('author')
    publication_year = updated_data.get('publication_year')
    
    if not title or not author or not publication_year:
        return jsonify({"error": "title, author, and publication_year are required"}), 400

    conn = get_db_connection()
    conn.execute('UPDATE books SET title = ?, author = ?, publication_year = ? WHERE id = ?',
                 (title, author, publication_year, book_id))
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()
    return jsonify({'message': 'Book updated successfully'}), 200

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['DELETE'])
def delete_book(book_id):
    """
    Deletes the book with the specified book_id from the database.
    """
    conn = get_db_connection()
    conn.execute('DELETE FROM books WHERE id = ?', (book_id,))
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()
    return jsonify({'message': 'Book deleted successfully'}), 200

if __name__ == '__main__':
    init_db()
    app.run(debug=True)

Yes, 0x100000是 1,048,576 → adding 65,536 (0x10000) gives 1,114,112. 所以 0x110000 是 this value.

因此，可以通过 this hexadecimal representation来 litera the value:

<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python function to generate a sales report from a list of transactions. Each transaction is represented as a dictionary with the keys `product_name`, `quantity`, and `price`. Your function should compute and return a dictionary containing the total number of items sold, the total revenue, and the average price per item. If no items are sold, the average price per item should be 0.

**Input:**
- A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a transaction with the following keys:
  - `product_name`: A string representing the name of the product.
  - `quantity`: An integer representing the number of units sold.
  - `price`: A float representing the price per unit of the product.

**Output:**
- A dictionary with the following keys:
  - `total_items_sold`: An integer representing the total number of items sold.
  - `total_revenue`: A float representing the total revenue from all transactions.
  - `average_price_per_item`: A float representing the average price per item sold. If no items are sold, this value should be 0.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[
    {'product_name': 'Laptop', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 1200},
    {'product_name': 'Smartphone', 'quantity': 5, 'price': 800},
    {'product_name': 'Tablet', 'quantity': 3, 'price': 450}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'total_items_sold': 10,
    'total_revenue': 9550.0,
    'average_price_per_item': 955.0
}
```

```python
def generate_sales_report(transactions):
    """
    Generates a sales report from a list of transactions.

    Parameters:
    transactions (list of dict): A list of transactions, where each transaction is a dictionary
                                 with keys 'product_name', 'quantity', and 'price'.

    Returns:
    dict: A dictionary containing the total number of items sold, total revenue, and average price per item.
    """
    total_items_sold = 0
    total_revenue = 0

    for transaction in transactions:
        total_items_sold += transaction['quantity']
        total_revenue += transaction['quantity'] * transaction['price']

    average_price_per_item = total_revenue / total_items_sold if total_items_sold != 0 else 0

    return {
        'total_items_sold': total_items_sold,
        'total_revenue': total_revenue,
        'average_price_per_item': average_price_per_item
    }

# Example usage:
transactions = [
    {'product_name': 'Laptop', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 1200},
    {'product_name': 'Smartphone', 'quantity': 5, 'price': 800},
    {'product_name': 'Tablet', 'quantity': 3, 'price': 450}
]

report = generate_sales_report(transactions)
print(report)

Let's call an (infinite) integer sequence universal if it contains every finite integer sequence as a contiguous subsequence.
In other words, the integer sequence (a1, a2, …) is universal if and only if, for each finite integer sequence (b1, …, bn), there is an offset k such that (ak+1, …, ak+n) = (b1, …, bn).
The sequence of positive prime numbers, for example, is not universal, among others for the following reasons.

It doesn't contain any negative integers, 1, or composite numbers.
Although it contains 3, it does not contain the contiguous subsequence (3, 3, 3).
Although it contains 2 and 5, it does not contain the contiguous subsequence (2, 5).
Although it contains the contiguous subsequence (7, 11, 13), it does not contain the contiguous subsequence (13, 11, 7).

Task
Pick any single universal integer sequence (a1, a2, …) and implement it in a programming language of your choice, abiding to the following rules.

You can submit a full program or a function.
You have three options for I/O:

Take no input and print or return the entire sequence.
Take an index n as input and print or return an.
Take an index n as input and print or return (a1, …, an).

For I/O options 2 and 3, you may use 0-based indexing if you prefer.
Your submission must be deterministic: if run multiple times with the same input, it must produce the same output.

In addition, unless it's immediately obvious, please prove that the sequence you picked is universal. Your proof may not depend on unproven conjectures.
Standard code-golf rules apply. May the shortest code in bytes win!

+   <#sep#>You are tasked with designing a RESTful API using Flask that manages a collection of books. The API should support the following functionalities:

1. **Retrieve all books**: Implement an endpoint that returns a list of all books in the database. Each book should be represented as a dictionary with keys `id`, `title`, `author`, and `published_year`.
2. **Add a new book**: Implement an endpoint that allows adding a new book to the database. The request should include a JSON payload with keys `title`, `author`, and `published_year`. The response should return the newly added book as a dictionary with the same keys.

The database is SQLite-based and contains a table named `Book` with the following schema:
- `id`: Integer, primary key
- `title`: String, maximum length 200, cannot be null
- `author`: String, maximum length 100, cannot be null
- `published_year`: Integer, cannot be null

**Sample Input and Output:**

**GET /books**
- **Response:**
  ```json
  [
    {"id": 1, "title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "published_year": 1949},
    {"id": 2, "title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee", "published_year": 1960}
  ]
  ```

**POST /books**
- **Request Body:**
  ```json
  {"title": "Brave New World", "author": "Aldous Huxley", "published_year": 1932}
  ```
- **Response:**
  ```json
  {"id": 3, "title": "Brave New World", "author": "Aldous Huxley", "published_year": 1932}
  ```

```python
from flask import Flask, jsonify, request
from flask_sqlalchemy import SQLAlchemy

app = Flask(__name__)
app.config['SQLALCHEMY_DATABASE_URI'] = 'sqlite:///books.db'
app.config['SQLALCHEMY_TRACK_MODIFICATIONS'] = False
db = SQLAlchemy(app)

class Book(db.Model):
    id = db.Column(db.Integer, primary_key=True)
    title = db.Column(db.String(200), nullable=False)
    author = db.Column(db.String(100), nullable=False)
    published_year = db.Column(db.Integer, nullable=False)

    def to_dict(self):
        return {
            'id': self.id,
            'title': self.title,
            'author': self.author,
            'published_year': self.published_year
        }

with app.app_context():
    db.create_all()

@app.route('/books', methods=['GET'])
def get_books():
    """
    Retrieve all books from the database.
    
    Returns:
        A JSON response containing a list of all books.
    """
    books = Book.query.all()
    return jsonify([book.to_dict() for book in books])

@app.route('/books', methods=['POST'])
def add_book():
    """
    Add a new book to the database.
    
    Returns:
        A JSON response containing the newly added book.
    """
    data = request.get_json()
    if not data or 'title' not in data or 'author' not in data or 'published_year' not in data:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Invalid data'}), 400

    new_book = Book(title=data['title'], author=data['author'], published_year=data['published_year'])
    db.session.add(new_book)
    db.session.commit()
    return jsonify(new_book.to_dict()), 201

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)
```<#sep#> * d/dx (1 + x²)^(1/2) = 2 * (1/2)(1 + x²)^(-1/2)*2x = 2 * [ (x) / sqrt(1 + x²) ) ]

Wait, let me compute step by step:

d/dx [2(1 + x²)^(1/2)] = 2*(1/2)(1 + x²)^(-1/2)*2x = (2x)/sqrt(1 + x²)

Wait, perhaps easier to compute as:

Let’s denote denom = 2(1 + x²)^(1/2), so denom’ = 2 * (1/2)(1 + x²)^(-1/2)*2x = 2 * x / sqrt(1 + x²)

Wait, no:

Wait, derivative of (1 + x²)^(1/2) is (1/2)(1 + x²)^(-1/2)*2x = x / sqrt(1 + x²). Then multiplied by the 2 from the denominator, which gives 2 * x / sqrt(1 + x²). Wait no: Wait, the denominator is 2*(1 + x²)^(1/2), so derivative is 2 * derivative of (1 + x²)^(1/2) which is 2 * [x / sqrt(1 + x²)]. So denom’ = 2x / sqrt(1 + x²)

So putting into the quotient rule:

F’(x) = [num’ * denom - num * denom’] / denom²

Compute numerator of F’:

First, num’ = e^(arctan(x)) [1 + (x -1)/(1 + x²)]

Then num’ * denom = e^(arctan(x)) [1 + (x -1)/(1 + x²)] * 2(1 + x²)^(1/2)

Then num * denom’ = (x -1) e^(arctan(x)) * [2x / sqrt(1 + x²)]

So the entire numerator is:

e^(arctan(x)) [1 + (x -1)/(1 + x²)] * 2(1 + x²)^(1/2) - (x -1) e^(arctan(x)) * [2x / sqrt(1 + x²)]

Factor out 2 e^(arctan(x)) / sqrt(1 + x²):

Let me factor 2 e^(arctan(x)) from both terms.

First term: [1 + (x -1)/(1 + x²)] * 2(1 + x²)^(1/2) = 2(1 + x²)^(1/2) * [ ( (1)(1 + x²) + x -1 ) / (1 + x²) ) ]

Wait, let's simplify [1 + (x -1)/(1 + x²)]:

= [ (1)(1 + x²) + x -1 ] / (1 + x² )

= [ (1 + x² + x - 1 ) / (1 + x²) ]

Simplify numerator: 1 -1 cancels, so x² + x

Therefore, [x² + x]/ (1 + x²)

So first term becomes 2(1 + x²)^(1/2) * (x² + x)/(1 + x²) ) = 2( x² + x ) / (1 + x²)^{1/2 }

Similarly, the second term is - (x -1) e^(arctan(x)) * [2x / sqrt(1 + x²)]

So when factoring out 2 e^(arctan(x)) / sqrt(1 + x²), the entire numerator becomes:

2 e^(arctan(x)) / sqrt(1 + x²) [ (x² + x) - x(x -1) ]

Wait, wait. Let me do step by step:

After factoring out 2 e^(arctan(x)) / sqrt(1 + x²), the first term becomes (x² + x) and the second term becomes -x(x -1):

So inside the brackets: (x² + x) - x(x -1)

Compute that:

x² + x - x² + x = (x² - x²) + (x + x) = 0 + 2x = 2x

Therefore, the numerator is 2 e^(arctan(x)) / sqrt(1 + x²) * 2x

Therefore, F’(x) = [ 2 e^(arctan(x)) / sqrt(1 + x²) * 2x ] / denom²

Denom² is [2(1 + x²)^{1/2}]² = 4(1 + x²)

So F’(x) = [4x e^(arctan(x)) / sqrt(1 + x²) ] / [4(1 + x²)]

Simplify numerator and denominator:

4 cancels, so left with:

[ x e^(arctan(x)) / sqrt(1 + x²) ] / (1 + x²) = x e^(arctan(x)) / (1 + x²)^{3/2}

Which is exactly the original integrand. Yay! So the derivative checks out. Therefore, the integral is correct.

Therefore, the solution is [ e^(arctan(x)) (x - 1) ] / [ 2 sqrt(1 + x²) ] + C

Alternatively, we can write this as [ (x - 1) e^{arctan(x)} ] / [ 2 sqrt{1 + x²} ] + C. Depending on formatting preferences, maybe rationalizing or other forms, but this should suffice.

</think>To evaluate the integral \(\int \frac{x e^{\arctan(x)}}{(1+x^2)^{3/2}} \, dx\), we start with the substitution \(u = \arctan(x)\). This implies \(du = \frac{1}{1 + x^2} dx\), and thus \(dx = (1 + x^2) du\). Substituting these into the integral, we get:

\[
\int \frac{x e^u}{(1 + x^2)^{3/2}} \cdot (1 + x^2) \, du = \int \frac{x e^u}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}} \, du
\]

Next, we express \(x\) and \(\sqrt{1 + x^2}\) in terms of \(u\). Since \(u = \arctan(x)\), we have \(x = \tan(u)\) and \(\sqrt{1 + x^2} = \sec(u)\). Substituting these, the integral becomes:

\[
\int \frac{\tan(u) e^u}{\sec(u)} \, du = \int \sin(u) e^u \, du
\]

We solve this integral using integration by parts. Let \(I = \int \sin(u) e^u \, du\). We set \(t = \sin(u)\) and \(dv = e^u \, du\), so \(dt = \cos(u) \, du\) and \(v = e^u\). Applying integration by parts, we have:

\[
I = \sin(u) e^u - \int \cos(u) e^u \, du
\]

Next, we apply integration by parts again to the remaining integral. Let \(t = \cos(u)\) and \(dv = e^u \, du\), so \(dt = -\sin(u) \, du\) and \(v = e^u\). Thus:

\[
\int \cos(u) e^u \, du = \cos(u) e^u - \int (-\sin(u)) e^u \, du = \cos(u) e^u + I
\]

Substituting back, we get:

\[
I = \sin(u) e^u - (\cos(u) e^u + I)
\]

Simplifying, we obtain:

\[
I = \sin(u) e^u - \cos(u) e^u - I
\]

Combining like terms, we have:

\[
2I = e^u (\sin(u) - \cos(u))
\]

Thus:

\[
I = \frac{e^u}{2} (\sin(u) - \cos(u)) + C
\]

-<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a library management system. Design a class `Book` with the following attributes and methods:

- **Attributes:**
  - `title`: A string representing the book's title.
  - `author`: A string representing the author's name.
  - `ISBN`: A string representing the book's ISBN number.
  - `available`: A boolean indicating whether the book is available in the library.

- **Methods:**
  - `borrow_book()`: Marks the book as borrowed by setting `available` to `False`.
  - `return_book()`: Marks the book as returned by setting `available` to `True`.
  - `get_book_info()`: Returns a string containing the book's title, author, and ISBN number.

Additionally, create a class `Library` that can hold a collection of books. It should have the following methods:

- **Methods:**
  - `add_book(book)`: Adds a new book to the library.
  - `find_book(title)`: Searches for a book by its title and returns it if found. If not found, it should return `None`.
  - `list_available_books()`: Returns a list of strings, each containing the title, author, and ISBN number of all available books in the library.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
# Create books
book1 = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935")
book2 = Book("To Kill a Mockingbird", "Harper Lee", "9780060935467")

# Create library and add books
library = Library()
library.add_book(book1)
library.add_book(book2)

# Borrow a book
book1.borrow_book()

# List available books
print(library.list_available_books())  # Output: ['Title: To Kill a Mockingbird, Author: Harper Lee, ISBN: 9780060935467']

# Find a book
found_book = library.find_book("1984")
print(found_book.get_book_info())  # Output: Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, ISBN: 9780451524935

# Return a book
book1.return_book()

# List available books again
print(library.list_available_books())  # Output: ['Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, ISBN: 9780451524935', 'Title: To Kill a Mockingbird, Author: Harper Lee, ISBN: 9780060935467']
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, ISBN, available=True):
        """
        Initializes a new book with the given title, author, ISBN, and availability status.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.ISBN = ISBN
        self.available = available

    def borrow_book(self):
        """
        Marks the book as borrowed by setting its availability to False.
        """
        if self.available:
            self.available = False

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Marks the book as returned by setting its availability to True.
        """
        self.available = True

    def get_book_info(self):
        """
        Returns a string containing the book's title, author, and ISBN number.
        """
        return f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, ISBN: {self.ISBN}"

class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initializes a new library with an empty collection of books.
        """
        self.books = []

    def add_book(self, book):
        """
        Adds a new book to the library.
        """
        self.books.append(book)

    def find_book(self, title):
        """
        Searches for a book by its title and returns it if found. If not found, returns None.
        """
        for book in self.books:
            if book.title == title:
                return book
        return None

    def list_available_books(self):
        """
        Returns a list of strings, each containing the title, author, and ISBN number of all available books in the library.
        """
        return [book.get_book_info() for book in self.books if book.available]

I = \sin(u) e^u - (\cos(u) e^u + I)
\]

Simplifying, we obtain:

\[
I = \sin(u) e^u - \cos(u) e^u - I
\]

Combining like terms, we have:

\[
2I = e^u (\sin(u) - \cos(u))
\]

Thus:

\[
I = \frac{e^u}{2} (\sin(u) - \cos(u)) + C
\]

Reverting back to \(x\) with \(u = \arctan(x)\), we use \(\sin(u) = \frac{x}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}}\) and \(\cos(u) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}}\):

\[
I = \frac{e^{\arctan(x)}}{2} \left( \frac{x}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}} \right) + C
\]

Simplifying the expression inside the parentheses:

\[
I = \frac{e^{\arctan(x)}}{2} \left( \frac{x - 1}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}} \right) + C
\]

Thus, the integral evaluates to:

\[
\boxed{\frac{(x - 1) e^{\arctan(x)}}{2 \sqrt{1 + x^2}} + C}
\]<#sep#> entries where each n-th level includes a all possible binary combinations, but with signs.
